Alternating POc reconstructions sometimes reflect alternations in PMP verbal morphology ([§1.3.5](../contributions/5-4#s-1-3-5)), and this appears to be the most complex case we have encountered. POc _&ast;kisu_/_&ast;kusu_ evidently reflects a PMP verb, for which, however, no non-Oceanic evidence is known. POc _&ast;ŋisu_/_&ast;ŋusu_ reflects the intransitive (actor voice) form of the verb, formed by replacing the initial _&ast;k-_ of the root with the homorganic nasal _&ast;ŋ-_ ([§1.3.5](../contributions/5-4#s-1-3-5).6). PWOc _&ast;kamisu_/_&ast;kimusu_ appears to reflect an alternative form of the intransitive, formed by infixing _⟨um⟩_ after the initial consonant of the root, giving expected POc †_&ast;k⟨um⟩isu_ or †_&ast;k⟨um⟩usu_. Finally, the set _&ast;kanisu_/_&ast;kanusu_/_&ast;kanusi_/_&ast;kinusu_ appears to reflect a nominalisation formed by infixing _⟨in⟩_ after the initial consonant of the root, giving expected POc †_&ast;k⟨in⟩isu_ or †_&ast;k⟨in⟩usu_, the latter reflected in Tabar _kinocu_. The _&ast;-a-_ vowel in POc _&ast;kanisu_/_&ast;kanusu_/_&ast;kanusi_ and PWOc _&ast;kamisu_ is taken to reflect epenthesis, as the infixes were almost certainly not stressed in POc, giving forms like _&ast;[k(ə)nisu]_ and _&ast;[k(ə)misu]_ (cf. Kokota and Maringe _kmisu_).

The second family of forms includes POc _&ast;supa_ and _&ast;ka-supa_, where _&ast;ka-_ appears to reflect the PMP formative _&ast;ka-_ found in stative intransitive verbs ([§1.3.5](../contributions/5-4#s-1-3-5).4). However, this is uncertain, as _&ast;ka-supa_ is not stative in meaning. The Nakanai, Boumaa Fijian and Micronesian reflexes of _&ast;ka-supa_ point to a transitive with an unexplained root-final _&ast;-t_.[^34]: Here again there are variant forms, but we have no explanation for these: the forms listed below _&ast;ka-supa[t-i]_ under ‘cf. also’ reflect a replacement of _&ast;-s-_ by _&ast;-n-_ (Roinji), by _&ast;-t-_ (Nakanai) or _&ast;-(r,R)-_ (Bulu, Bola, Misima).

One further term _&ast;puRuk_ ‘spray water from the mouth; spray a mixture of saliva and masticated medicinal herbs on an ailing body part in curing’ is included with supporting evidence in [§5.4.2](../contributions/5-4#s-5-4-2).1.


<a id="s-4-4"></a>

### 4.4. Vomiting


Two POc terms have been reconstructed, _&ast;[mu]mutaq_ and _&ast;luaq_. A number of languages (Lou, Gela, Longgu, To’aba’ita, Kwaio) have reflexes in both sets. Their glosses suggest that _&ast;[mu]mutaq_ simply meant ‘vomit’, whereas _&ast;luaq_ denoted forceful ejection of a substance from the body, as discussed below.

POc _&ast;mutaq_ reflects PMP _&ast;um-utaq_, i.e. the root preceded by the intransitive actor voice affix ([§1.3.5](../contributions/5-4#s-1-3-5).5).

<a id="p-285"></a>

The Lou, Mangap, Gela, Kwaio and Samoan terms suggest that POc _&ast;luaq_ may have implied a more forceful action of vomiting or spitting, perhaps also including the ejaculation of seminal fluid as in To’aba’ita and Rennellese. As with the verbs of secretion and excretion discussed in [§4.4](../contributions/5-4#s-4-4), the transitive form in _&ast;-i_ took a location as its object (_&ast;luaq-i_ ‘vomit on’), and the transitive form in _&ast;-akin[i]_ marked the product as the object (_&ast;luaq-akin[i]_ (VT) ‘vomit s.t. up’).

<a id="p-286"></a>


<a id="s-4-5"></a>

### 4.5. Ejaculation of seminal fluid


No separate term for ejaculation of seminal fluid can be reconstructable, but it seems possible that this was one of the senses of POc &ast;luaq above ([§4.4.4](../contributions/5-4#s-4-4-4)), as this is one of the meanings of the To’aba’ita and Rennellese reflexes of the latter.


<a id="s-4-6"></a>

### 4.6. Sweating, perspiring and perspiration


POc _&ast;maqono_ ‘sweat’ appears from its form (_&ast;ma-_ + disyllabic root) to have originally been a verb, but a number of its reflexes, often reduplicated, are now nouns. The absence or presence of final _&ast;-ta_, reflected in ’Are’are and Maewo, is unexplained.

No extra-Polynesian cognates of PPn _&ast;ka-kawa_ have been found.

<a id="s-4-7"></a>

### 4.7. Urinating and urine


PMP _&ast;miqmiq_ ‘urinate, urine’ seems to have had two POc reflexes, (i) the expected form _&ast;mimiq_ and (ii) a form _&ast;mimi(s)_ in which _&ast;-q_ was replaced by _&ast;-s_. The final _&ast;-s_ surfaces only in the transitive form _&ast;mimis-i-_ ‘urinate on’, and it is of course possible that some of the reflexes assigned to _&ast;mimi(s)_ below in which no final consonant is retained should be assigned to _&ast;mimiq_ and vice versa.

Assignments of forms in which no final consonant is retained are made on the basis of the geographic distribution of reflexes of the two forms. Forms that reflect _&ast;-q_ are located in NNG and PT languages, in the northernmost subgroup of Meso-Melanesian, namely Tungag-Nalik of northern New Ireland, and in scattered NCV languages of Malakula. Forms that reflect _&ast;-s_ are found in Nakanai (MM, Willaumez), in several SE Solomonic languages, in a number of NCV languages from Ambae and Malakula, and in Bauan and Wayan Fijian. Admiralties forms have all lost the final consonant, but are assigned to _&ast;mimiq_ on the assumption that Proto Admiralty separated early from the rest of Oceanic and is more likely to have preserved the conservative form _&ast;mimiq_. All other forms that have lost the final consonant occur in the region of _&ast;mimis-i-_ forms and are assumed to reflect _&ast;mimi(s)_.

But there are complications. Several languages assumed to reflect _&ast;mimiq_ actually reflect a variant _&ast;memeq_. They are Mussau, Mumeng, Kapin, Lala and Bali—well scattered. A number of South New Ireland languages and Proto NW Solomonic, assumed to reflect _&ast;mimi(s)_, usually retain POc final consonants, but the relevant reflexes below all have the form _mimi_, suggesting that the final consonant was irregularly lost in these languages. And finally a number of North/Central Vanuatu and New Caledonian languages reflect PSOc _&ast;meRe-_ ‘urine’, _&ast;[me]meRe_ ‘urinate’, _meRes-i-_ ‘urinate on’.[^37]: This seems to be a variant of POc _&ast;mimi(s)_ ‘urinate’, _&ast;mimis-i-_ ‘urinate on s.t.’ which replaces the root _&ast;mimis_ with the root _&ast;meRes_.[^38]:


<a id="p-288"></a>

It is difficult to offer an explanation of this variation, other than to suggest that euphemism may have led to wordplay. But this does not account for the fact that almost all the forms mentioned above occur in NCV languages, and that closely related languages have in some cases inherited different forms.

The set of forms below has no known cognates outside New Guinea Oceanic (NNG, PT) and raises several puzzles. If the PNGOc etymon was a trisyllable with final -CV, then its reduction to a disyllable in Wab, Bing and Numbami is puzzling. It would be explained if Sio busali were originally a transitive verb reflecting suffix _&ast;-i_ ‘urinate on’. The PNGOc intransitive root ‘urinate’ would then have been _&ast;bʷaju(r,R)_,[^40]: the transitive _&ast;bʷaju(r,R)-i_, but the PT forms in final _-u_ do not support this reconstruction, as the default vowel added after an inherited final consonant in Suauic languages (i.e. Tubetube and Suau) is _-i_, not the attested _-u_. Furthermore, the sets of forms listed under ‘cf. also’ appear to reflect PPT _&ast;bʷasoso_ and PPT _&ast;bʷasi_ and their relationship to the reconstructed form is not understood.

<a id="p-291"></a>


<a id="s-4-8"></a>

### 4.8. Defecating


Like POc _&ast;mimiq_, reflexes of POc _&ast;pekas_ are generally intransitive, but can be transitivised when additional information is included (_&ast;pekas-i_ ‘defecate on s.t.’, _&ast;pekas-aki[ni]_ ‘defecate s.t.’). POc _&ast;taqe_ was primarily a noun, ‘faeces’, but is reflected as a verb in some languages (see [§3.8.6](../contributions/5-4#s-3-8-6)).

When more than one term is reconstructable, one may be used as a polite term. e.g. in Tikopia _tiko_ is the regular word, while _peka_ is used in the presence of kin with whom constraint of relationship is observed.

<table class="cognateset" id="5-4-4-8-291-POc-pekas-a">
<tr>
<td><strong>POc</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;pekas</i>
</td>
<td>'<span>defecate; faeces</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>POc</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;pekas-i</i>

<a id="p-292"></a>

A number of languages use reflexes of POc _&ast;tape_ ‘to flow’ ([vol.2:93](../contributions/2-4#p-93)), commonly referring to the flow of blood or other liquids, but sometimes referring to excretory functions (Gela _tave toba_ ‘diarrhoea’, Mota _tatave_ ‘to excrete’).


<a id="s-5"></a>

## 5. Respiration and events involving the respiratory organs



<a id="s-5-1"></a>

### 5.1. Breathing


POc _&ast;[ma]ñawa_ ‘breathe’ is among a small group of experiential POc verbs beginning with the prefix _&ast;ma-_ where the intransitive subject is a human experiencer ([Evans 2003](../sources/Evans2003):276; [§1.3.5](../contributions/5-4#s-1-3-5).4). Other _&ast;ma-_ initial verbs discussed here include POc _&ast;[ma]soru_ ‘hiccup’, POc _&ast;mawap_ ‘yawn’, and _&ast;[ma]turu(R)_ ‘sleep, to be asleep’.


<a id="p-293"></a>

There is a great deal of variation in the English glosses of reflexes, but much of it falls into place when it is recognised that the inherited core meaning of POc _&ast;[ma]ñawa_ was something like ‘living essence, soul’ of a human being, which included breathing and the beating heart as the physical manifestation of life. ‘Rest’ follows from this: cf. English ‘take a breather’.

Occasional reflexes of POc _&ast;[ma]ñawa_ include reference to the fontanelle. This is a visible pulse in a young baby, reflecting the fact that the baby is alive.

Proto Polynesian has two reflexes of POc _&ast;[ma]ñawa_, namely PPn _&ast;mānawa_ ‘breathe; breath’ and PPn _&ast;manawa_ ‘belly’. In consequence it is tempting to reconstruct a pair of POc (near-)homophones, but this would probably be a mistake, as the contrast between short and long vowels is a Central Pacific (Fijian and Polynesian) phenomenon. PPn _&ast;mā-_ marked an undergoer-subject intransitive verb, and _&ast;mānawa_ was a verb meaning ‘breathe’. The nominal ‘breath’ gloss represents a derivation. PPn _&ast;manawa_ on the other hand was a noun, ‘belly’.

The two Polynesian sets are kept separate below, even though the reflexes of the two forms are identical in several (especially Eastern Polynesian) languages where the long/short vowel distinction has been lost in this pair of etyma.

<a id="s-5-2"></a>

### 5.2. Gasping and panting


There are no well populated cognate sets for ‘gasp’ or ‘pant’, but there are enough data to allow two reconstructions. Only the first, POc _&ast;oŋap_ ‘pant, be out of breath’, has known non-Oceanic cognates, and only its Gela reflex is problem-free. Other reflexes display the irregularities indicated in parentheses, and the first three lack a reflex of initial POc _&ast;o-_.

<table class="cognateset" id="5-4-5-2-295-POc-oap-a">
<tr>
<td><strong>PMP</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;eŋap</i>
</td>
<td>'<span>gasp for breath</span>' (<a href="../sources/Blust1995">ACD</a>)
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>POc</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;oŋap</i>
</td>
<td>'<span>pant, be out of breath</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>NNG</td><td><a href="../languages/kove">Kove</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>ŋave</i></td>
<td>'<span>pant, as a dog</span>' (<span><em>-e</em> unexplained</span>)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>NNG</td><td><a href="../languages/kaulong">Kaulong</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>ŋep</i></td>
<td>'<span>pant</span>' (<span><em>-e-</em> for †<em>-a-</em></span>)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>MM</td><td><a href="../languages/sursurunga">Sursurunga</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>ŋeh-ŋeh</i></td>

</tr>
<tr>
<td>MM</td><td><a href="../languages/tolai">Tolai</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>ŋua</i></td>
<td>'<span>asthma</span>' (<span>metathesis</span>)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>MM</td><td><a href="../languages/roviana">Roviana</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>uŋa</i></td>
<td>'<span>asthma, bronchitis</span>' (<span><em>u-</em> for †<em>o-</em></span>)</td>
</tr>

<td>SES</td><td><a href="../languages/gela">Gela</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>oŋa</i></td>

</tr>
</table>




<a id="p-296"></a>

The other reconstruction is POc _&ast;ŋaRa_, which, despite various meaning extensions in its reflexes, appears to have meant ‘be breathless, pant’ and to have included asthmatic panting.

<i>&ast;ŋaRa</i>

<a id="s-5-3"></a>

### 5.3. Snoring


Probably POc _&ast;ŋorok_ ‘grunt, growl, snore’ reflects the same root as _&ast;ŋorok_ ‘snot’ ([§3.8.3](../contributions/5-4#s-3-8-3)). A reduplicated form _&ast;ŋoro-ŋorok_ means ‘channel above upper lip’ ([§3.4.12](../contributions/5-4#s-3-4-12)).

<a id="p-297"></a>


<a id="s-5-4"></a>

### 5.4. Blowing air from the mouth


Several POc verbs of blowing are reconstructable, but most of these refer to the blowing of the wind, and are presented in [vol.2:125](../contributions/2-5#p-125)–127. The only POc verbs which we can say with some confidence denoted a person blowing air from the mouth are the pair _&ast;ipu_ and _&ast;upi_, which probably have a common origin in PMP _&ast;ibut_ ‘breeze, draught of wind’. We can be reasonably confident about their meaning because their reflexes are used of playing traditional flutes and by extension for the flutes themselves ([vol.1:107](../contributions/1-4#p-107)–108).

<a id="s-5-5"></a>

### 5.5. Gaping


Three reconstructions with the same root, POc _&ast;(q)aŋap_, denote the notion of gaping or having one’s mouth wide open. In the first set below the root occurs by itself. The other two reflect the affixes _&ast;⟨um⟩_ ([§1.3.5](../contributions/5-4#s-1-3-5).5) and _&ast;paN-_ ([§1.3.5](../contributions/5-4#s-1-3-5).6), both forming dynamic intransitive verbs.

<i>&ast;qaŋa[p,b]</i>

<a id="s-5-6"></a>

### 5.6. Yawning


A single cognate set embraces all of Oceanic and indeed all the Austronesian family. Blust (ACD) writes:

> Irregular reflexes of PAn _&ast;Suab_ are quite common, particularly in the Oceanic languages. The cognation of such Oceanic forms as Seimat maw (where only -/w/ remains from the original stem) with Western Malayo-Polynesian forms such as Kelabit _uab_ is clear from the fairly abundant intermediate forms that reflect PAn _&ast;ma-Suab_ ( > _ma-huab_ > _ma-uab_ > _mawab_).

> A perfect parallel is seen in PAn _&ast;ma-Seyaq_, POc _&ast;mayaq_ ‘shy, ashamed’. In both cases the boundary between the stative prefix _&ast;ma-_ and the stem has been lost in all CEMP witnesses. The only non-CEMP language in which a similar loss of morpheme boundary has taken place is Chamorro (with _magap_, for expected _&ast;&ast;magwap_ ‘yawn’). Following the reanalysis of _&ast;ma-uab_ as _&ast;mawab_ a number of CEMP languages have either introduced a new stative marker, or have reduplicated the first syllable of the new stem. It remains unclear how many of these added syllables (if any) are the result of convergent developments (ACD).

Reduplicated forms of the kind Blust refers to are shown separately below the main cognate set.

<a id="s-5-7"></a>

### 5.7. Coughing


It is difficult to detect any difference in meaning between POc _&ast;koso_, POc _&ast;pukuR_/PROc _&ast;puRuk_ and PWOc _&ast;kuk(a,u)_. Rather, POc _&ast;pukuR_ appears to be synonymous with _&ast;koso_ and to have won out in the Bel group (part of North New Guinea) and in much of SE Solomonic. POc &ast;kuk(a,u) is intriguing: few reflexes are found, but their distribution indicates a POc origin.

<a id="s-5-8"></a>

### 5.8. Sniffing and blowing one’s nose


Two terms are reconstructed, POc _&ast;s(i,u)r(i,u)(t)_ ‘sniff, blow nose’ and POc _&ast;paŋus_, _&ast;paŋus-i-_ ‘blow one’s nose’. The latter almost certainly bears a historical relationship to PAn _&ast;Siŋus_/PMP _&ast;hiŋus_ ‘sniff, sniffle (as with a runny nose)’ (ACD) and to the terms for ‘snot’ listed at the end of [§3.8.3](../contributions/5-4#s-3-8-3), but it is not clear what the (morphological) relationship is.

<a id="p-305"></a>


<a id="s-5-9"></a>

### 5.9. Sneezing


Forms for ‘sneeze’ can be divided into two main groups:

- a western group embracing the Admiralties, Western Oceanic and SE Solomonic, reflecting
  putative onomatopoeic POc forms such as ?_&ast;(k)asipeŋ_, POc ?_&ast;(k)asiŋe(k)_ or ?_&ast;(k)asio_;
- an eastern group embracing Vanuatu and Micronesia, reflecting PROc _&ast;mʷat(i,u)a_
  ‘sneeze’.

In addition there are numerous cognate sets too local in extent to be included here.

The reconstructions associated with the western group are prefixed by a question mark because (i) the data support several formally similar but distinct reconstructions; and (ii) the data contain a good many irregularities (shown in parentheses below) in relation to normal sound correspondences. Both phenomena suggest that onomatopoeia (sound symbolism) has been at work, sometimes resisting the effects of regular sound change, sometimes making otherwise arbitrary changes in forms. For example, the MM reflexes below of POc ?_&ast;(k)asipeŋ_ point to _&ast;(k)atipeŋ_, i.e. _&ast;-s-_ was at some point replaced by _&ast;-t-_. The Gela, Lengo and Longgu reflexes below reflect unpredicted loss of _&ast;(k)a-_ and Gela and Lengo reflect apparent resistance to the sound change that lenited _&ast;-s-_ to _-h-_ or _-ð-_. In each case the innovation (or lack of one) is restricted to a small area.

If the Mussau and Titan forms below are indeed cognate with the NNG forms (the correspondences are regular), then POc ?_&ast;(k)asiŋe(k)_ can be tentatively reconstructed. All the NNG forms reflect _-s-_, the fortis reflex of POc _&ast;-s-_, where a lenis reflex (in languages from Lukep to Ham either _-y-_ or -∅-) is expected. This seems to be an instance of resistance to sound change for the sake of onomatopoeia.

What kind of historical relationship exists between POc ?_&ast;(k)asio_ below and POc ?_&ast;(k)asipeŋ_ and POc ?_&ast;(k)asiŋe(k)_ above is a matter for speculation. POc ?_&ast;(k)asio_ is self-evidently onomatopoeic. Again _-s-_, the fortis reflex of POc _&ast;-s-_, is found where a lenis reflex is expected. Precisely because of its sound symbolism, it is uncertain whether the form existed in POc.

<a id="p-307"></a>

The set below is also onomatopoeic, and it is open to debate whether the Meso-Melanesian and Polynesian forms reflect a single POc form or are the result of parallel independent innovations. The Nakanai forms are added because they illustrate effects of onomatopoeia. Superficially they appear to belong to this set, but Nakanai _-h-_ reflects POc _&ast;-q-_, found in none of the forms reconstructed above. Whatever their origins, the Nakanai forms must have acquired _-h-_ through sound symbolism, not by cognacy with forms in any of the western sets presented here.

<a id="p-308"></a>


<a id="s-6"></a>

## 6. Sleeping and waking



<a id="s-6-1"></a>

### 6.1. Sleeping


One POc term for sleeping is reconstructed, _&ast;[ma]turu(R)_ ‘sleep, be asleep’, alongside two PWOc terms, _&ast;[ma]puta_ ‘sleep’ and PWOc _&ast;mataip_ ‘be fast asleep’. PEOc _&ast;mo(q)e_ ‘be fast asleep’and PCP &ast;moze ‘sleep’ are also discussed below.

POc _&ast;[ma]turu(R)_ (VI) ‘sleep, to be asleep’ contains the _&ast;ma-_ stative prefix that indicates that the subject is a human experiencer ([Evans 2003](../sources/Evans2003):276). Only Bali and Blablanga (MM) reflect the root _&ast;tuduR_ alone, whilst Bola, Nakanai and Meramera (all Willaumez languages) add mata ‘eye’ to the root to form ‘sleepy’ ([§4.6.2](../contributions/5-4#s-4-6-2).1).

No reflexes of POc _&ast;[ma]turu(R)_ are found in New Guinea Oceanic (NNG, PT) languages. In these it is fairly consistently replaced by reflexes of _&ast;qenop_ ‘lie’ ([§6.2.3](../contributions/5-4#s-6-2-3)), its meaning extended to include both ‘lie’ and ‘sleep. The converse meaning extension whereby a reflex of _&ast;[ma]turu(R)_ comes also to mean ‘lie’ is much rarer but is reflected in three New Ireland languages below: Lavongai, Tigak and Tabar.

How PWOc _&ast;[ma]puta_ differed in meaning from POc _&ast;[ma]turu(R)_ is not clear. Curiously, the seemingly cognate PPn verb &ast;ma-futa meant ‘start up, arise, awake from sleep’. Whether the apparent cognacy is real—with an odd reversal of meaning—or simply a chance resemblance remains unclear.

<a id="s-6-2"></a>

### 6.2. Being tired


A number of languages distinguish between ‘sleepy’ (‘eye tired’) and ‘weary’ or ‘exhausted’ (‘body tired’) through the use of body-part metaphors.

<table id="5-4-6-2-310-adm-seimat-pulalihian">

<td><a href="../languages/seimat">Seimat</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>pula lihian  </i></td>
<td>[his.eye tired]</td>
<td>
'<span>sleepy</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Adm</td>
<td><a href="../languages/seimat">Seimat</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>tinu nohan   </i></td>
<td>[his.skin tired]</td>
<td>
'<span>exhausted, weary</span>'</td>
</tr>

<td><a href="../languages/kilivila">Kilivila</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>imama mati-la</i></td>

<td>
'<span>he is weary</span>'</td>

<td><a href="../languages/kilivila">Kilivila</a></td>

'<span>he is utterly weary</span>'</td>

<a id="s-6-2-1"></a>

#### 6.2.1. Being sleepy


In widely distributed Oceanic languages the idiomatic way of saying ‘I am sleepy’ is a phrase meaning ‘my eyes are sleeping’. In the examples below, the reflex of POc _&ast;mata-_ ‘eye’ is shown with a following hyphen because it takes a possessor suffix (reflecting _&ast;-gu_ ‘my’, _&ast;-mu_ ‘your.S’, _&ast;-ña_ ‘her/his’ etc). The verb in each case is glossed ‘sleep’.

<table id="5-4-6-2-310-nng-mangap-matapot">
<tr>
<td>NNG</td>
<td><a href="../languages/mangap">Mangap</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>mata- pot     </i></td>
<td>
'<span>be sleepy, slacken, abate, wane</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>PT</td>
<td><a href="../languages/gumawana">Gumawana</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>mata- i-masisi</i></td>
<td>
'<span>be sleepy</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>MM</td>
<td><a href="../languages/nehan">Nehan</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>mata- hohou   </i></td>
<td>
'<span>be sleepy</span>'</td>
</tr>
</table>


<a id="p-311"></a>

In a number of languages the phrasal expression has evolved into a compound.

<td><a href="../languages/misima">Misima</a></td>

'<span>sleepiness</span>'</td>

<tr>

<td><a href="../languages/nakanai">Nakanai</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>mata-tu-tulu</i></td>

'<span>sleepy</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td><a href="../languages/meramera">Meramera</a></td>

'<span>sleepy</span>' (<span>for †<em>mā-mauru</em></span>)</td>

'<span>sleepy</span>' (<span>for †<em>mā-mauruʔa</em></span>)</td>

'<span>sleepy</span>'</td>

In yet other languages a different verb is used with ‘eye’, either in a phrase or a compound. The meaning of that verb follows these examples.

<table id="5-4-6-2-311-pt-tawala-matapota">

<td><a href="../languages/tawala">Tawala</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>mata-pota</i></td>
<td>[eye-shut]</td>
<td>
'<span>sleepy</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>PT</td>
<td><a href="../languages/motu">Motu</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>mata ɣara    </i></td>
<td>[eye burn]</td>
<td>
'<span>sleepy</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>SES</td>
<td><a href="../languages/kwaio">Kwaio</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>mā- e olo-olo</i></td>
<td>[eye- it roll.around]</td>
<td>
'<span>sleepy</span>'</td>
</tr>

<td>NCV</td>
<td><a href="../languages/mota">Mota</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>mata-maraɣai</i></td>
<td>[eye-quiver]</td>
<td>
'<span>sleepy</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>NCV</td>
<td><a href="../languages/lewo">Lewo</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>mara- kawa </i></td>

<td>
'<span>sleepy</span>'</td>
</tr>
</table>

And in other languages the meaning of the verb is not given independently in the dictionary. In some languages it is glossed ‘sleepy’, suggesting that an earlier independent meaning may have been lost.

A number of languages use a phrase meaning ‘wants to sleep’ for ‘sleepy’. Arosi has a desiderative prefix gasi that can be used before any verb, hence gasi mauru ‘sleepy’ [want sleep]. Some Central Pacific languages do the same thing with a reflex of the desiderative particle _&ast;via_ ([§11.5](../contributions/5-4#s-11-5)) and a term for ‘sleep’ (cf. ‘wants to eat’ for ‘hungry’ and ‘wants to drink’ for ‘thirsty’; §§4.3.3.1–2).

<table class="cognateset" id="5-4-6-2-311-PCP-viamoze-a">
<tr>
<td><strong>PCP</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;via moze</i>
</td>
<td>'<span>sleepy</span>' (<span>lit. ‘want sleep’</span>)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Fij</td><td><a href="../languages/bauanfijian">Bauan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>via moðe  </i></td>
<td>[VI] '<span>sleepy</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/tongan">Tongan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>fie mohe-a</i></td>
<td>[VI] '<span>be sleepy</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/marquesan">Marquesan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>hia moe   </i></td>
<td>'<span>sleepy</span>'</td>
</tr>
</table>

'<span>be sleepy</span>' (<span><em>mata-</em> ‘want’</span>)</td>
</tr>
</table>



<a id="p-312"></a>


<a id="s-6-2-2"></a>

#### 6.2.2. Being weary, exhausted


Oceanic languages commonly have different terms to express different kinds of bodily tiredness, e.g. ‘weak’, ‘fatigued’, ‘exhausted’. The Tolai and Bugotu dictionaries, for instance, each list five terms with general meaning ‘tired, weary’. Body part metaphors also make fine distinctions in meaning, e.g.

<table id="5-4-6-2-312-nng-yabem-lipopo">
<tr>
<td>NNG</td>
<td><a href="../languages/yabem">Yabem</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>ʊli popoʔ</i></td>
<td>[his.body shattered]</td>
<td>
'<span>he is exhausted, worn out (after hard work)</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>NNG</td>
<td><a href="../languages/yabem">Yabem</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>ʊli kɪtuŋ</i></td>
<td>[his.- body burns]</td>
<td>
'<span>his body aches, is worn out, exhausted</span>'</td>
</tr>
</table>

POc, PCP and PPn terms are reconstructed.

POc _&ast;malu[malumu]_ ‘weak, tired’ is a partial reduplication of POc _&ast;[ma]lumu_ ‘soft, gentle, easy’ ([vol.2:215](../contributions/2-7#p-215)). The Polynesian reflexes show irregular loss of _&ast;-m-_.

<table class="cognateset" id="5-4-6-2-312-POc-malumalumu-a">
<tr>
<td><strong>POc</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;malu[-malumu]</i>
</td>
<td>'<span>weak, tired</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>MM</td><td><a href="../languages/ramoaaina">Ramoaaina</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>malu-malum</i></td>
<td>'<span>weak, faint, sick</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>MM</td><td><a href="../languages/tolai">Tolai</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>[mal-]malu</i></td>
<td>'<span>weak, tired, of a part of the body</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>MM</td><td><a href="../languages/patpatar">Patpatar</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>mal-malu(ŋo)</i></td>
<td>'<span>tired from working; lazy</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Fij</td><td><a href="../languages/bauanfijian">Bauan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>malu-malumu</i></td>
<td>'<span>weak, faint, sick, soft</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/tongan">Tongan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>molū</i></td>
<td>'<span>soft, tender, flexible</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/niuean">Niuean</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>molū</i></td>
<td>'<span>soft, weak, humble</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/pukapukan">Pukapukan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>malū</i></td>
<td>'<span>weak, gentle</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/tikopia">Tikopia</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>malūlū</i></td>
<td>'<span>weary; soft, weak, flabby</span>' (<span><em>-l-</em> for †<em>-r-</em></span>)</td>
</tr>
</table>

cf. also: 
<table>
<tr>
<td>SES</td>
<td><a href="../languages/arosi">Arosi</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>marō</i></td>
<td>
'<span>weary, bodily tired</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>NCV</td>
<td><a href="../languages/mota">Mota</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>male</i></td>
<td>
'<span>weak</span>'[^41]</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>NCV</td>
<td><a href="../languages/namakir">Namakir</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>molo-mal</i></td>
<td>
'<span>lazy</span>'</td>
</tr>
</table>


PCP _&ast;wai-wai_ ‘weak, tired’ is apparently a reduplicated reflex of POc _&ast;waiR_ ‘river, fresh water, stream’ ([§1.3.5](../contributions/5-4#s-1-3-5).4). The gloss of Wayan wai-ðala ‘be weak (of kava etc), diluted, melt, dissolve’ points to the connection between water and weakness.

<table class="cognateset" id="5-4-6-2-312-PCP-waiwai-a">
<tr>
<td><strong>PCP</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;wai-wai</i>
</td>
<td>'<span>weak, tired</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Fij</td><td><a href="../languages/rotuman">Rotuman</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>vai-vai</i></td>
<td>'<span>flexible, easily bent</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>PPn</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;wai-wai</i>
</td>
<td>'<span>weak, lacking strength</span>' (<span>POLLEX</span>)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/tongan">Tongan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>vaivaia</i></td>
<td>[VI] '<span>feel weak</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/samoan">Samoan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>vāivai</i></td>
<td>'<span>be weak; be tired; be timid, faint-hearted; watery (of a mix); faint, of colours</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/tokelauan">Tokelauan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>vāivai</i></td>
<td>'<span>tiredness, weariness, exhaustion</span>'</td>
</tr>
</table>



PPn _&ast;fītaqa_ ‘be tired, fatigued’ evidently reflects POc &ast;pita ‘heavy, difficult’, reconstructed below, with the addition of an apparent suffix _&ast;-qa_ (which may reflect the POc adjectivaliser _&ast;-ka_; [Ross 2000](../sources/Ross2000)).


<a id="p-313"></a>

<a id="s-6-3"></a>

### 6.3. Dreaming


The two POc forms _&ast;nipi_ and _&ast;mipi_, both ‘dream’, reflect fossilised combinations of a reflex of the PMP root _&ast;hipi_ ‘dream’ and the PMP voice affixes _&ast;⟨in⟩_ and _&ast;⟨um⟩_ ([§1.3.5](../contributions/5-4#s-1-3-5).5). The fact that both forms are reflected, interspersed with one another across a substantial part of Oceania, indicates that the two forms co-existed in POc.

</tr>

<a id="p-315"></a>


<a id="s-6-4"></a>

### 6.4. Waking up and opening the eyes


The POc transitive verb _&ast;paŋun_ ‘wake (s.o.) up’ is of PAn antiquity and has reflexes throughout Oceanic.

<table class="cognateset" id="5-4-6-4-315-POc-paun-a">
<tr>
<td><strong>PAn</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;baŋuL</i>
</td>
<td>[VI] '<span>wake up, get out of bed</span>' (<a href="../sources/Blust1995">ACD</a>)
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>PMP</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;baŋun</i>
</td>
<td>[VT] '<span>wake (s.o.) up, rouse (s.o.) from sleep</span>' (<a href="../sources/Blust1995">ACD</a>)
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>POc</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;paŋun</i>
</td>
<td>[VT] '<span>wake (s.o.) up</span>' (<a href="../sources/Blust1995">ACD</a>)
</td>
</tr>

<a id="p-316"></a>

A Proto Central Pacific intransitive verb, _&ast;qadra_ ‘awaken, be awake’, is reconstructable, but no POc intransitive verb with this sense has been identified. The reason seems to be that an expression meaning ‘open the eyes’ is used in many languages for ‘wake up’.

<table class="cognateset" id="5-4-6-4-316-PCP-qadra-a">
<tr>
<td><strong>PCP</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;qadra</i>
</td>
<td>[VI] '<span>awaken, be awake</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>PCP</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;qadrav-i-</i>
</td>
<td>[VT] '<span>keep watch over</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Fij</td><td><a href="../languages/bauanfijian">Bauan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>yadra</i></td>
<td>[VI] '<span>open the eyes, wake up</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Fij</td><td><a href="../languages/bauanfijian">Bauan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>yadrav-a</i></td>
<td>[VT] '<span>watch for</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Fij</td><td><a href="../languages/wayan">Wayan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>adra</i></td>
<td>[VI] '<span>wake up</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Fij</td><td><a href="../languages/wayan">Wayan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>adravi-</i></td>
<td>[VT] '<span>stand watch over s.t.</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>PPn</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;qara</i>
</td>
<td>[VI] '<span>wake up, (be) awake</span>' (<span>POLLEX</span>)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/tongan">Tongan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>ʔā</i></td>

</tr>

<td>'<span>keep watch over (a corpse) at night</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/samoan">Samoan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>ala</i></td>
<td>'<span>be awake</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/rennellese">Rennellese</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>ʔaga</i></td>

</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/tahitian">Tahitian</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>ara</i></td>
<td>'<span>awake</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/takuu">Takuu</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>ara</i></td>
<td>'<span>be awake</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/eastfutunan">East Futunan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>ʔala</i></td>

<td>Pn</td><td><a href="../languages/hawaiian">Hawaiian</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>ala</i></td>

</table>

cf. also: 
<table>
<tr>
<td>Pn</td>
<td><a href="../languages/niuean">Niuean</a></td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>ala</i></td>
<td>
[VI] '<span>to wake, be awake</span>' (<span>loan from a Nuclear Polynesian language</span>)</td>
</tr>
</table>


If Lau ada ‘open the eyes, use the eyes’ belonged to this set, _&ast;(q)adra_ could be reconstructed to PEOc. However, the regular Lau reflex of _&ast;(q)adra_ would be _sada_ with prothetic _s-_ following loss of _&ast;q-_, and Lau _ada_ appears instead to be a regular reflex of POc _&ast;tadraq_ ‘look up, see’ ([§8.2](../contributions/5-4#s-8-2))).

Polynesian languages use reflexes of POc _&ast;Ropok_ ‘fly’ ([§6.3.2](../contributions/5-4#s-6-3-2).1; [vol.4:281](../contributions/4-6#p-281)) to describe the event that occurs when one is suddenly surprised or woken.

<td><strong>POc</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;Ropok</i>
</td>

</tr>
</table>

PMP _&ast;bilat_ ‘open the eyes’ has only one known reflex in Oceanic.

<a id="s-6-5"></a>

### 6.5. Blinking and closing the eyes


This section is concerned with the momentary action of closing and then opening the eye(s) as in winking or blinking, rather than the initial stage of going to sleep. There is a PMP etymon _&ast;kimet_, whose Oceanic reflexes are evidently restricted to Central Pacific languages.

<tr>
<td><strong>PMP</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;kimet</i>
</td>
<td>'<span>blink, flash</span>' (<a href="../sources/Blust1986">Blust 1986</a>)
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>POc</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;kimo</i>
</td>
<td>'<span>blink, wink</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>POc</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;kimo-kimo</i>
</td>
<td>'<span>keep blinking or winking</span>'</td>
</tr>

<td>Fij</td><td><a href="../languages/wayan">Wayan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>kimo-mo</i></td>

<a id="s-7"></a>

## 7. Physical responses to emotion, pain or cold



<a id="s-7-1"></a>

<a id="p-319"></a>


<a id="s-7-2"></a>

### 7.2. Grinning


Grinning is closely associated in Oceanic languages with baring the teeth, and in a number of languages the term for ‘tooth’ is derived from the term for ‘grin’. POc _&ast;sisi_, _&ast;ŋiŋi_ and _&ast;ŋisi_ each have a PMP antecedent. The history of POc _&ast;[ŋi]ŋisa_ is discussed below.

Oceanic languages often appear to lack a dedicated verb for smiling. Instead, verbs for ‘laugh’ or ‘grin’ are sometimes additionally glossed ‘smile’.

<table class="cognateset" id="5-4-7-2-319-POc-sisi-a">
<tr>
<td><strong>PMP</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;ziziq, &ast;zizir</i>
</td>
<td>'<span>grin, show the teeth</span>' (<a href="../sources/Blust1995">ACD</a>)
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>POc</strong></td><td> </td>
<td style="white-space: nowrap;">
<i>&ast;sisi</i>
</td>
<td>'<span>smile, show one’s teeth, bare one’s teeth</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>NNG</td><td><a href="../languages/gedaged">Gedaged</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>sisi</i></td>
<td>'<span>draw up (the lips, as when smiling)</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>NNG</td><td><a href="../languages/manam">Manam</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>(ao)sisi</i></td>
<td>'<span>to smile, of long duration</span>' (<span><em>ao</em> ‘smile, laugh’, <em>sisi</em> ‘skin the bark off a tree’</span>)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>NNG</td><td><a href="../languages/mato">Mato</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>(ma)sisi</i></td>
<td>'<span>laugh</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>SES</td><td><a href="../languages/tolo">Tolo</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>sisi(la)</i></td>
<td>'<span>smile; show one’s teeth</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>SES</td><td><a href="../languages/saa">Sa’a</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>sisi</i></td>
<td>'<span>roll back, grin like a dog, lay bare the teeth</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>SES</td><td><a href="../languages/arosi">Arosi</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>sisi</i></td>
<td>'<span>lay bare the teeth, as a dog, grinning</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>NCV</td><td><a href="../languages/mota">Mota</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>sis</i></td>
<td>'<span>remove rind or bark; strip off outer part</span>'</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Fij</td><td><a href="../languages/bauanfijian">Bauan</a></td><td style="white-space: nowrap;"><i>(vaka)sisi(bati)</i></td>
<td>'<span>smile, show the teeth</span>' (<span><em>bati</em> ‘tooth’</span>)</td>
</tr>
</table>



Blust (ACD) reconstructs both PWMP _&ast;ŋiŋi_ ‘grin, show the teeth’ and PMP _&ast;ŋisi_ ‘grin, show the teeth’. PMP _&ast;ŋiŋi_ and _&ast;ŋisi_ are evidently the antecedents of POc _&ast;ŋiŋi_ and _&ast;ŋisi_ below. The data also require the reconstruction of POc _&ast;[ŋi]ŋisa_. Any attempt to combine any two of the three sets fails, as we are compelled to posit irregular developments, and the existence of non- Oceanic cognates of both POc _&ast;ŋiŋi_ and POc _&ast;ŋisi_ confirms that both should be reconstructed. The presence of three formally similar POc terms with the same meaning may appear suspect, but the evidence requires their reconstruction, even though their reflexes have perhaps been conflated in some languages. Some reflexes could be attributed to more than one set, and we have made attributions as best we can on the basis of phonology and glosses.

The meaning ‘tooth’ could also be attributed to each of these reconstructions, but as there were other POc terms for teeth ([§3.4.12](../contributions/5-4#s-3-4-12).5) that do not include the sense ‘bare the teeth’, ‘tooth’ may be a secondary meaning that has arisen independently in various daughter-languages.

<a id="s-7-3"></a>

### 7.3. Weeping and crying


Two sets of POc terms are reconstructed for ‘cry, weep’:

- _&ast;taŋis_ (VI) ‘cry, lament etc.’, _&ast;tanis-i-_ (VT) ‘to cry for (s.t.’), _&ast;tanis-aki[ni]-_ ‘cry because of s.t.’
- _&ast;ŋara(s)_ (VI) ‘cry’, (VT) _&ast;ŋaras-i-_ ‘cry for (s.t.)’


<a id="p-321"></a>

POc _&ast;taŋis_ appears to have been the default term. The glosses of both cognate sets suggest that their primary meaning had to do with the sound of crying rather than the shedding of tears, and the glosses of reflexes of POc _&ast;ŋara(s)_ suggest that it denoted weeping accompanied by very loud crying.

<a id="s-7-4"></a>

### 7.4. Grunting and moaning


Verbs of grunting, groaning, moaning and the like appear often to be local onomatopoeic innovations, so that cognate sets barely exist and, even where we find putative cognates, there is a possibility of independent parallel innovation. It is possible that some members of the set below do not reflect POc _&ast;[ŋuk]ŋuk_ ‘grunt, moan’ but are independent coinages.

<a id="s-7-5"></a>

### 7.5. Goosebumps


No POc reconstruction has been made, but a number of terms for goosebumps have been collected which identify it with thorns or prickles of plants, barbs of sago leaf or spikes of spiny fish. The Wayan Fijian and Niuean terms support reconstruction of a Proto Central Pacific term.

<td>
'<span>pricks, short protuberance, gooseflesh</span>' (<span>POc <em>*(dr,r)uRi</em> ‘thorn’; vol.3:125</span>)</td>
</tr>

<td>NNG</td>
<td><a href="../languages/dami">Dami</a></td>

</tr>

<td style="white-space: nowrap"><i>losaka-sakalulu</i></td>

<a id="s-7-6"></a>

### 7.6. Trembling and shivering


There were a number of POc terms for ‘tremble, shiver’. Six of these fall into three pairs. One member of each pair has the skeleton _&ast;rVrV_, the other _&ast;drVdrV_. The pairs are _&ast;riri_ (with variant _&ast;ridriŋ_) and _&ast;dridri_, _&ast;rere_ and _&ast;dredre_ (with variant _&ast;drere_), and _&ast;ruru_ and _&ast;drudru_.[^42]:

The fact that there are three pairs of forms is intriguing, and the _&ast;rere_/_&ast;dredre_ and _&ast;ruru_/_&ast;drudru_ pairs appear to have arisen in POc, perhaps through onomatopoeic wordplay.

Of these roots, only _&ast;riri_/_&ast;ridriŋ_ has possible non-Oceanic cognates, and its history and variation in form are discussed in [§4.8.1](../contributions/5-4#s-4-8-1), as it also occurs in terms for ‘be cold’. Suffice it to say here that the original form of the root was probably POc _&ast;ridriŋ_, but this is reflected in ‘tremble’ forms only in Mangap and Sio. Elsewhere, assimilation has occurred and widespread reflexes of _&ast;riri_ are found, suggesting that this was already an alternant in POc. Only Sio and Marshallese reflect _&ast;dridri_.

The most widely reflected POc term for ‘tremble, shiver’ is _&ast;riri_/_&ast;ridriŋ_. Since this root also occurred in terms for ‘be cold’, one might infer that its earliest meaning was perhaps ‘shiver’, but the glosses of reflexes below suggest that it was used both for trembling with fear and for shivering with cold or a fever.

The POc pair _&ast;rere_ and _&ast;dredre_ ‘tremble, shiver’ appear to be an alternant version of the pair _&ast;riri_ and _&ast;dridri_. This is plausible formally, as POc _&ast;e_ only reflects PMP _&ast;-ay_, i.e. in inherited items it occurs only morpheme-finally. Medial POc _&ast;-e-_ is either the outcome of borrowing from a non-Oceanic language or of idiosyncratic innovation. We suggest that it is due to the latter here. Nowhere are both _&ast;rere_ and _&ast;riri_ reflected in a single language, and there is no evident contrast in meaning, beyond the fact that reflexes of _&ast;rere_ have acquired the meaning ‘fear’ in Bel languages (Bilibil etc.) and in Fijian. The Takia phrase _tini-g i-rer_ ‘my skin trembles’ [skin-my it-tremble] shows how the extension of meaning occurred.

<a id="p-326"></a>

The second member of this pair was apparently POc _&ast;dredre_ (with variant _&ast;drere_), but PPn _&ast;tete_ raises a question of form. Was its POc ancestor _&ast;dredre_ or _&ast;dede_? By regular sound change it was _&ast;dede_, but the likely history of the root _&ast;ridriŋ_, reflecting PMP _&ast;diŋdiŋ_ ([§4.8.1](../contributions/5-4#s-4-8-1)), points to POc _&ast;dr_, not _&ast;d_. We cannot resolve this conflict, but note that the POc consonant _&ast;d_ was rare medially and almost non-existent initially, suggesting that _&ast;dredre_ was modified to _&ast;dede_ in an early Oceanic dialect ancestral to PPn.

<a id="p-327"></a>

The PNCV reflex of POc _&ast;ruru_ (VI) ‘shake’ had acquired the additional sense of ‘earthquake’ ([vol.2:82](../contributions/2-3#p-82)), but there is no evidence of this elsewhere, and the POc pair _&ast;ruru_ and _&ast;drudru_ do not seem to have differed in meaning from the two pairs above. The form _&ast;drudru_ is reflected only in Remote Oceanic languages. The Polynesian forms may reflect either _&ast;ruru_ or _&ast;drudru_.

<a id="p-328"></a>

In [vol.2:80](../contributions/2-3#p-80), POc _&ast;ninir_ ‘earthquake’ was reconstructed. Like PNCV _&ast;ruru_ above, this seems to have reflected a verb meaning ‘tremble, shake’. The final _&ast;-r_ is not reflected in the non-Oceanic cognates from which PMP &ast;ninih is reconstructed, and the sets supporting PMP &ast;ninih and POc _&ast;ninir_ may resemble each other by chance.

<a id="s-8"></a>

## 8. Temperature


The reconstruction of terms for ‘hot’ and ‘cold’ is tricky, because the English words have a number of senses for which Oceanic speakers use different words or phrases. The English use of ‘cold’ to denote a sickness is set aside here. Some Oceanic languages distinguish between

a. a person feeling cold (the sense [§4.8.1](../contributions/5-4#s-4-8-1) is concerned with),
b. a substance being cold to the touch, and
c. the air, wind or weather being cold.

Thus in To’aba’ita the terms are (a) _tega_ or _aqai_, (b) _gʷari_, and (c) _ʔoleʔoleʔa_. However, there is a twist: (a) may also be _gʷa-gʷari_, a reduplicated form of (b) ([Lichtenberk 2008](../sources/Lichtenberk2008)).

In Dobu (a) and (c) are both _gogai_, and (b) is _gonituna_, or _goyuyuwana_ if the cold substance is water ([Lithgow & Lithgow 2006](../sources/LithgowandLithgow2006)).

Very few of the available dictionaries differentiate the senses of ‘cold’ as carefully as these two, so there is a data problem. This is compounded, as seen in both To’aba’ita and Dobu, by the fact that a term may span two of the three senses. Much the same is true of words for ‘hot’.


<a id="p-329"></a>

Although it may be assumed that POc made distinctions of the kind listed above, it is not possible to determine accurately how reconstructed terms for ‘hot’ and ‘cold’ related to (a), (b) and (c).


<a id="s-8-1"></a>

### 8.1. Feeling cold


There are several POc forms for ‘cold’ which are derived ultimately from PMP _&ast;diŋin_ ‘cold’. However, Chamorro _maniŋhiŋ_ ‘cold’ reflects PMP _&ast;maN-diŋdiŋ_ ([Blust 1970](../sources/Blust1970):133), and it can be assumed that the POc forms reflect the PMP reduplicated root _&ast;diŋdiŋ_. The expected POc form of the root is _&ast;ridriŋ_, which is plentifully reflected, but this has been subject to assimilations and perhaps metathesis at various post-POc interstages. The root alone is reflected as POc _&ast;ridri(ŋ)_, or _&ast;riri(ŋ)_ ‘shiver’ (with assimilation of medial _&ast;-dr-_ to initial _&ast;r-_), discussed in [§7.6](../contributions/5-4#s-7-6).

Forms for ‘cold’ consist of one of the prefixes _&ast;ma-_, _&ast;maN-_ and _&ast;maka-_ + &ast;ridriŋ, giving expected POc forms _&ast;ma-ridriŋ_, _&ast;madridriŋ_ (from _&ast;maN-ridriŋ_) and _&ast;maka-ridriŋ_, all of which are reflected in present-day Oceanic languages. Matters are complicated by the fact that forms are also found which appear to reflect _&ast;ma-ririŋ_, _&ast;madririŋ_, i.e. with medial _&ast;-dr-_ replaced by _&ast;-r-_. There are several possible explanations of these forms, including assimilation, dissimilation and metathesis, but these are ignored here as their distribution in the data suggests that they are local innovations. This means that in a few cases forms reflecting _&ast;ma-ridriŋ_ may have been attributed to _&ast;madridriŋ_, and vice versa.

It is possible that the forms here attributed to POc _&ast;madridriŋ_ are actually all reflexes of _&ast;ma-ridriŋ_ that have undergone assimilation of root-initial _&ast;-r-_ to medial _&ast;dr-_. However, their wide distribution speaks against this. As _&ast;madridriŋ_ reflects _&ast;maN-ridriŋ_, and _&ast;maN-_ has an agentive implication, the question arises, How could a term for ‘cold’ be agentive? The answer is perhaps that it denoted coldness of weather, and there is a sense in which weather can be regarded as agentive (‘causing shivering’): ‘cold (of weather)’ is the gloss assigned to PMP _&ast;maN-diŋdiŋ_ by [Zorc](../sources/Zorc2007) ([2007](../sources/Zorc2007)).

We take _&ast;ma-ridriŋ_ and _&ast;maka-ridriŋ_ both to have meant ‘feel cold’, but their meanings may have been wider than this. No semantic difference between them is discernible.

<a id="s-8-2"></a>

### 8.2. Feeling hot


Three terms are reconstructable for ‘hot, warm’, POc _&ast;[ma]panas_, POc _&ast;maŋini(t)_ and POc _&ast;tunu-tunu_. The first was probably the general term, to judge from its distribution and its glosses, whilst _&ast;maŋini(t)_ probably had some specialised sense. The third was apparently derived from the verb POc _&ast;tunu_ ‘roast on embers or in fire’ ([vol.1:293](../contributions/1-9#p-293)).

## Notes

[^1]: This reduplicated usage appears to reflect lexicalisation of reduplication that in POc encoded and in many Oceanic languages encodes the imperfective aspect.

[^2]: The root _&ast;susu_ also formed the directly possessed noun ‘breast, milk’ ([§3.5.7](../contributions/5-4#s-3-5-7)).

[^3]: The expected PMP form is †_&ast;paŋaen_, but all known reflexes point to _&ast;paŋan_.

[^4]: Reconstruction of the form _&ast;koda(q)_ is required by the Woleaian, Pulo Annian and Fijian reflexes.

[^5]: Neither source recognised the presence of POc _&ast;kʷ_. On this, see [Ross](../sources/Ross2011) ([2011](../sources/Ross2011)).

[^6]: Neither is reflected in the Papuan Tip cluster, the third section of Western Oceanic.

[^7]: _&ast;qusi_ is reconstructed here rather than _&ast;qusu_ because _&ast;-i_ was added to verb roots in POc to form transitive verbs. Nehan _uhu_ is assumed to reflect loss of _&ast;-i_ followed by echo-vowel addition.

[^8]: [Geraghty](../sources/Geraghty1990) ([1990](../sources/Geraghty1990)) assigns Nakanai liu ‘drink’ to POc _&ast;iRup_ ‘sip (as soup), slurp’ but [Bender et al.](../sources/Benderetal2003) ([2003](../sources/Benderetal2003):29) point out that it reflects POc _&ast;inum_.

[^9]: However, [Blust](../sources/Blust1993) ([1993](../sources/Blust1993), [2009b](../sources/Blust2009b)) argues that PCEMP/POc _&ast;unum_ should be reconstructed alongside PCEMP/ POc _&ast;inum_ because the lack of western Malayo-Polynesian forms reflecting _&ast;unum_ implies that such forms in CEMP languages are not the result of local changes but of the innovation of a PCEMP variant _&ast;unum_.

[^10]: Oceanic specialists will recognise that this reconstruction entails one or perhaps two phonemes not usually reconstructed for Proto Oceanic (cf [§1.3.4](../contributions/5-4#s-1-3-4).1), namely _&ast;gʷ_ and _&ast;ŋʷ_. [Ross](../sources/Ross2011) ([2011](../sources/Ross2011)) adds to the Proto Oceanic consonant inventory the phoneme _&ast;kʷ_, with a decidedly low token frequency and a tendency to appear in contexts where a phonaesthetic factor is at play. It would not be surprising if _&ast;gʷ_ and _&ast;ŋʷ_ had also occurred, with even lower token frequency.

[^11]: Blust (ACD) does not reconstruct the sense ‘suck (at the breast)’ for PMP _&ast;susu_, but it is clear from the data he cites that it can be reconstructed.

[^12]: Whereas PMP _&ast;-an_ survived as POc _&ast;-an_, a nominaliser, PMP _&ast;-en_ survived only as a fossil in a few nouns like _&ast;pitolon_ ([§1.3.5](../contributions/5-4#s-1-3-5).5, footnote 33).

[^13]: Anejom _(e)tele_ shows unexpected loss of POc initial _&ast;p-_: the expected form is †_(e)htele_ (John Lynch, pers. comm.)

[^14]: [Clark & Clark](../sources/ClarkandClark1995) ([1995](../sources/ClarkandClark1995)) gloss _rako-ña_ as an intransitive verb, but the presence of the 3SG possessor suffix _-ña_ suggests that it is or has been an adjective ([Ross 1998b](../sources/Ross1998b)).

[^15]: The source of Goodenough’s citation is unclear.

[^16]: _&ast;inem_ ‘drink’ is a rarely reflected variant of _&ast;inum_ ([§4.3.2](../contributions/5-4#s-4-3-2).1).

[^17]: The early Austronesian root for ‘sink’ was _&ast;-lem_, and forms derived from this occur in Oceanic languages: Nyindrou (Adm) _-lon_ ‘sink’, Nakanai (MM) _tilomu_ ‘sink, drown’, Sursurunga (MM) _lum_ ‘dive’,‘ _lum-i_ ‘dive to obtain or catch s.t.’, Longgu (SES) _lulumi_ ‘sink’.

[^18]: A final CVC syllable had two moras and accordingly received stress.

[^19]: &ast;[dn] could also have arisen though nasalisation, then extension of voicing, i.e. &ast;[tl] > &ast;[tn] > &ast;[dn].

[^20]: These forms have removed an initial _&ast;tn-_ cluster by deleting initial _&ast;t-_. Cf. footnote 22.

[^21]: Replacement of _&ast;t-_ by Psohoh _k-_ appears to be a case of cluster misinterpretation, noted in (3) earlier.

[^22]: These forms have removed an initial _&ast;tŋ-_ cluster by deleting initial _&ast;t-_. Cf. footnote 20.

[^23]: In certain PT languages _&ast;l_ and _&ast;ŋ_ have both become _n_, so these reflexes are ambiguous as to whether they reflect metathesis or not.

[^24]: In Misima _&ast;l_ and _&ast;ŋ_ have both become _n_. However, _&ast;ŋ_ is lost word-finally, whereas _&ast;l_ isn’t, so _tinon_ reflects PWOc _&ast;toŋol_.

[^25]: In Sissano and Sera n reflects both POc _&ast;n_ and _&ast;ŋ_.

[^26]: Note metathesis: _&ast;tonomo_ > _&ast;tonmo_ > _tomno_. This is probably borrowed from Bugotu.

[^27]: Bender et al. reconstruct PMic _&ast;Toromi_, but the data support _&ast;toromi_ equally well.

[^28]: _&ast;mʷ-_ is reconstructed to account for Mapos Buang mul.

[^29]: Blust (ACD) also cites Ponapean _metik_, _mitik_ ‘kiss’, but the final _-k_ suggests that this is not a native Ponapean term. This leaves no Oceanic evidence for reconstruction of _&ast;-k_.

[^30]: NCV data provided by John Lynch.

[^31]: Transitive forms of Longgu, NE Ambae and Boumaa Fijian in the following sets are from [Evans 2003](../sources/Evans2003):198.

[^32]: It is tempting to argue that _-usi_ includes a reflex of the transitive suffix _&ast;-i_, but the glosses do not support this, and the form would be irregular, the regular form being †_-isu-i_ or †_-usu-i_.

[^33]: We have chosen to treat Laghu _ki-knisu_ and Blablanga _na-pnisu_ as reflexes of an earlier _&ast;kmisu_, reflected in Kokota and Maringe, rather than of _&ast;kanisu_, but nothing depends on this decision.

[^34]: Non-etymological final consonants are often the result of reanalysis of POc _&ast;-C-i_ as _&ast;-Ci_, then generalisation of one productive variant of _&ast;-Ci_.

[^35]: Wayan _katasivi_ and its derivatives reflect a blend of _kata-_, which occurs in verbs denoting noises from the mouth (_kata-sīsī_ ‘hiss “pssst”’, _kata-som_ ‘signal with a squeaky kissing sound’) and PCP _&ast;kasivi_ ‘spit’ (Andrew Pawley, pers. comm.).

[^36]: Blust reconstructs this form as Proto Western Malayo-Polynesian, but we do not believe that this node existed ([vol.4, ch.1, §3.2.2](../contributions/4-1#s-3-2-2)). Instead, reconstructions at this interstage represent PMP items of which no CEMP reflexes were known at the time of reconstruction. The presence of Oceanic reflexes here confirms the PMP attribution.

[^37]: The New Caledonian items listed here do not reflect POc _&ast;s_ or _&ast;q_. John Lynch (pers. comm.) suggests that they should accordingly be assigned to _&ast;meRe-_, as they regularly lose _&ast;R_.

[^38]: Clark reconstructs _&ast;-r-_. We reconstruct _&ast;-R-_ because François (2011) has shown that _&ast;R_ tends more strongly to be lost the further south one moves in the NCV area, and _&ast;meRes_ adheres to this pattern.

[^39]: NCV forms are from John Lynch (pers. comm.).

[^40]: The indeterminacy of _&ast;-(r,R)_ is present in most PNGOc reconstructions that reflect POc _&ast;r_ or _&ast;R_ as the latter merged in most Western Oceanic languages.

[^41]: [Clark](../sources/Clark2009) ([2009](../sources/Clark2009)) reconstructs PNCV _&ast;malo~&ast;male_ ‘lazy, tired, weak’. The relationship between this and POc _&ast;malu[-malumu]_ is not clear.

[^42]: Of the six forms, only _&ast;ridriŋ_ has a reflex (Mangap _riŋriŋ_) with a final consonant.

[^43]: The only non-Oceanic cognate listed by [Blust](../sources/Blust1970) ([1970](../sources/Blust1970)) is (CMP) Roti _makalini_ ‘cold’, so the etymon is perhaps only of PCEMP antiquity.
